$\end{proof}\par NotethatonecannotencodeinfinitelybranchingMDPsintofinitelybranchingMDPsingeneral.E.g.,evenfor$\liminf_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$it\emph{does}makeadifferencewhethertheMDPisfinitelybranchingorinfinitelybranching;cf.~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:nestedcobuchi}and\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:liminf}.\par\vskip 12.0pt plus 4.0pt minus 4.0pt\begin{remark}If the transition rewards are restricted to the integers, then
the objective $\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$ just corresponds to the objective to visit
transitions with reward $\geq 0$ infinitely often, i.e.,
${\sf G}{\sf F}\,A$ where $A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\{t\in{\longrightarrow}\mid\ r(t)\geq 0\}$.
Thus, instead of $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$, it suffices to consider the
simpler B\"{u}chi objective ${\sf G}{\sf F}\,A$.
The strategy complexity of the B\"{u}chi objective in countable MDPs has been
studied in \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{KMSW2017,KMST:ICALP2019,KMST2020c}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
\par Optimal strategies for B\"{u}chi, where they exist, can be chosen as
Det(Positional), but this does not carry over to $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$,
where even Det(F) is not sufficient (\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:limsuplowerdetf}),
and instead optimal strategies can be chosen as Det(SC) or Rand(Positional); cf.~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:limsup}.
\par$\varepsilon$-optimal strategies for B\"{u}chi can be chosen as Det(SC + 1-bit),
but not as Rand(SC) or Rand(F).
The lower bounds trivially carry over to $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
The Det(SC + 1-bit) upper bound also carries over, but it requires a more complex
proof (\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:epsGF}).
\end{remark}\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Upper Bounds}
\par We choose to present the upper bounds in terms of
$\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$,
because it is more natural.
First we present the results for optimal strategies,
and then those for $\varepsilon$-optimal strategies.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Strategy Complexity of Optimal Strategies}
\par\begin{theorem}Let ${\mathcal{M}}=(S,S_{C},S_{R},{\longrightarrow},P,r)$ be a
universally transient countable MDP
with initial state $s_{0}$ with a
$\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ objective.
If there exists an almost surely winning strategy from $s_{0}$,
then there also exists an almost surely winning deterministic positional strategy.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}W.l.o.g.\ we can assume that ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching, and it
suffices to consider a sub-MDP where every state admits an almost surely
winning strategy.
A run satisfies $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$
iff it visits infinitely many transitions in $A_{i}$ for every $i\in\mathbb{N}$.
We partition the state space into infinitely many finite regions
in the shape of expanding rings around the initial state $s_{0}$,
where membership in each ring
is defined via certain lower and upper bounds on the length of the shortest path from
$s_{0}$.
Since $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}\subseteq{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ for all $i$,
every state has value one for the reachability objective ${\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
Since the rings are chosen sufficiently large,
we can fix a deterministic positional strategy inside the $i$-th ring
that ensures that $A_{i}$ is visited with probability $\geq 1/2$ already inside the
$i$-th ring whenever the $i$-th ring is entered from the previous $(i-1)$-th
ring.
Since our MDP is universally transient and every ring is finite, it follows
that almost all runs (except for a nullset) eventually reach the $i$-th ring for every $i$.
Hence, for every $i\in\mathbb{N}$ we obtain a $\geq 1/2$ chance of visiting $A_{i}$.
Since the sets $A_{i}$ form a monotone decreasing chain, we
satisfy $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ almost surely.
\end{proof}\par\begin{proof}W.l.o.g.\ we can assume that ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:branchingreplacement}.
Assume furthermore that there exists an almost surely winning strategy
$\sigma^{\prime}$ from $s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$.
Let $S^{\prime}\subseteq S$ be the subset of states that are visited
under $\sigma^{\prime}$. By restricting ${\mathcal{M}}$ to $S^{\prime}$, we obtain a sub-MDP
${\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$ where all states are almost surely winning for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
\par By construction of ${\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$, we have
$\forall s\in S^{\prime}\,{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}}(s)}=1$.
Note that ${\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}\subseteq{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ for all $i$.
Hence we have that ${\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},{\sf F}\,A_{i}}(s)}\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}}(s)}=1$ for all $s\in S^{\prime}$ and all $i\in\mathbb{N}$.
Let $d:S\to\mathbb{N}$ be a distance function where $d(s)$ is the length of the shortest path from $s_{0}$ to $s$.
Let ${\sf Bubble}_{n}(s_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\{s\in S^{\prime}\mid d(s)\leq n\}$.
\par We inductively construct
an increasing sequence of numbers $0=n_{0}<n_{1}<n_{2}<\dots$ and
a sequence of MDPs ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ for $i=0,1,2,\dots$ that are derived from ${\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$
by fixing choices in larger and larger finite subspaces ${\sf Bubble}_{n_{i}}(s_{0})$.
These will satisfy the following properties:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@For all $i\geq 0$, ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ is universally transient and
all states in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ are almost surely winning for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@For all $i,j\geq 1$ we have the following.
\par Let $S_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\sf Bubble}_{n_{i}}(s_{0})$,
$X_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}S_{i}\setminus S_{i-1}$ and
let
$A_{i}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\{(s_{1}{\longrightarrow}s_{2})\in A_{i}\mid s_{1},s_{2}\in X_{j}\}$
be the transitions in $A_{i}$ that happen completely inside the subspace $X_{j}$.
\par In ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$, under all strategies, for all $j\leq i$,
from any state $s\in S_{j}$ the probability of seeing a transition in
$A_{j+1}^{j+1}$ is $\geq 1/2$.
(This property will hold under all strategies, because in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ the relevant
choices will be fixed already.)
Formally, for all $j\leq i$ we have
\begin{equation}\forall\tau\ \forall s\in S_{j}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1},s,\tau}({\sf F}\,A_{j+1}^{j+1})\geq 1/2\end{equation}\end{itemize}\par In the base case $i=0$ we have ${\mathcal{M}}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$, $n_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}0$ and
$S_{0}=\{s_{0}\}$.
The first property holds by construction of ${\mathcal{M}}_{0}={\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$ and the second is
vacuously true (since it assumes $i\geq 1$).
\par Induction step $i{\longrightarrow}i+1$:
We define ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ inductively as follows.
\par Consider the MDP ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$.
Since ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ (like ${\mathcal{M}}$) is finitely branching,
the set $S_{i}$ is finite.
By induction hypothesis, all states in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ are almost surely winning
for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
Moreover, since ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ (like ${\mathcal{M}}$) is universally transient,
under all strategies,
the set of runs which stays within a finite set forever is a nullset.
In particular this holds for the finite set $S_{i}=\cup_{j\leq i}X_{j}$. Therefore
${\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i+1}$
is equal to
${\sf G}{\sf F}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j})$
up to a nullset (by universal transience) and we have
${\sf G}{\sf F}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j})\subseteq{\sf F}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j})$.
Thus every state in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ is also almost surely
winning for the reachability objective ${\sf F}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j})$.
\par In countable MDPs there exist uniform $\varepsilon$-optimal deterministic positional strategies for
reachability \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Ornstein:AMS1969}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
Since all states in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ have value $1$ for the reachability objective
${\sf F}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j})$,
we can pick a uniform deterministic positional strategy $\sigma_{i+1}$
that is $1/4$-optimal from every $s\in S_{i}$, i.e.,
$$\forall s\in S_{i}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i+1}}({\sf F}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j}))\geq 1-\dfrac{1}{4}=\dfrac{3}{4}.$$Since ${\sf F}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf F}^{\leq k}$,
by continuity of measures from below we have
$$\forall s\in S_{i}\,\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i+1}}({\sf F}^{\leq k}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j}))\geq\dfrac{3}{4}.$$Thus for all states $s\in S_{i}$ there is a $k(s)$
s.t.\ ${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i+1}}({\sf F}^{\leq k(s)}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j}))\geq 1/2$.
Since $S_{i}$ is finite,
$k_{i+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\max\{k(s)\mid s\in S_{i}\}$ is finite.
Hence
$$\forall s\in S_{i}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i+1}}({\sf F}^{\leq k_{i+1}}(A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j}))\geq 1/2.$$Let $n_{i+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}n_{i}+k_{i+1}$.
All transitions in $A_{i+1}\setminus\cup_{j\leq i}A_{i+1}^{j}$ that are reachable from
$S_{i}$ in $\leq k_{i+1}$ steps are contained in $A_{i+1}^{i+1}$
by the definitions of $n_{i+1}$, $X_{i+1}$ and $A_{i+1}^{i+1}$.
Thus
\begin{equation}\forall s\in S_{i}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i+1}}({\sf F}\,A_{i+1}^{i+1})\geq 1/2.\end{equation}We obtain ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ from ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ by fixing the (remaining) choices inside
$S_{i+1}={\sf Bubble}_{n_{i+1}}(s_{0})$ according to $\sigma_{i+1}$.
From \eqref{eq:T-iplus-iplus} and the definition of ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$
we obtain \eqref{eq:lem:ppalmostupper-1} for the case of $j=i$.
The other cases of \eqref{eq:lem:ppalmostupper-1} where $j<i$ follow from the induction hypothesis.
\par${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ is universally transient, since ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ is universally transient.
Thus only a nullset of runs stays in the finite set $S_{i+1}$ forever.
Thus, since $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ is shift invariant, even in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ all
states are almost surely winning for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
This concludes the induction step.
\par Let $\sigma^{*}$ be the deterministic positional strategy on ${\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$ that is compatible
with the MDPs ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ for all $i$.
I.e.\ $\sigma^{*}$ plays like $\sigma_{i}$ on $X_{i}$ for all $i$.
We'll show that $\sigma^{*}$ is almost surely winning for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ from
$s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$. To this end, we show that the dual objective
$\neg\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ is a nullset under $\sigma^{*}$.
\par We know that
\begin{equation}\neg\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf F}{\sf G}\,\neg A_{i}.\end{equation}\par By the definition of the sets $A_{i}^{j}$ we have
$\bigcup_{j\geq i}A_{j}^{j}\subseteq A_{i}$ and thus
$\neg A_{i}\subseteq\bigcap_{j\geq i}\neg A_{j}^{j}$.
Moreover, we cannot reach $A_{j}^{j}$ in fewer than $j$ steps from $s_{0}$.
Hence, for any $k\in\mathbb{N}$ we have
\begin{equation}{\sf F}^{\leq k}{\sf G}\,\neg A_{i}\subseteq{\sf F}^{\leq k}{\sf G}\,\left(\cap_{j\geq i}\neg A_{j}^{j}\right)\subseteq{\sf G}\,\left(\cap_{j>\max\{i,k\}}\neg A_{j}^{j}\right).\end{equation}\par Since ${\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$ (like ${\mathcal{M}}$) is universally transient, only a nullset of runs stays in a finite set forever, in particular each of the finite sets $S_{m}$
for every $m\in\mathbb{N}$.
Thus every run from $s_{0}$ must eventually reach the set $X_{m}$ for each
$m\in\mathbb{N}$.
This holds under every strategy, and thus in particular
under the strategy $\sigma^{*}$, i.e.,
\begin{equation}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},s_{0},\sigma^{*}}(\cap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\,{\sf F}\,X_{m})=1\end{equation}\par By the construction of $\sigma^{*}$, for any $i,k\in\mathbb{N}$ we obtain the following
\begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},s_{0},\sigma^{*}}({\sf G}\,(\cap_{j>\max\{i,k\}}\neg A_{j}^{j}))\\
={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},s_{0},\sigma^{*}}({\sf G}\,(\cap_{j>\max\{i,k\}}\neg A_{j}^{j})\cap\cap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf F}\,X_{m})&\text{by \eqref{eq:acyc-reach-Xi}}\\
\leq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},s_{0},\sigma^{*}}(\cap_{j>\max\{i,k\}}{\sf F}(X_{j-1}\cap{\sf G}\neg A_{j}^{j}))&\text{set inclusion}\\
\leq\prod_{j>\max\{i,k\}}\max_{s\in X_{j-1}}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},s,\sigma^{*}}({\sf G}\neg A_{j}^{j})&\text{$X_{j-1}$ finite}\\
=\prod_{j>\max\{i,k\}}\max_{s\in X_{j-1}}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{j},s,\sigma_{j}}(\neg{\sf F}\,A_{j}^{j})&\text{def.\ $\sigma^{*}$, ${\sf G}$}\\
\leq\prod_{j>\max\{i,k\}}1/2&\text{by \eqref{eq:T-iplus-iplus}}\\
=0.\end{array}\end{equation}Hence for all $i,k\in\mathbb{N}$,
\begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},s_{0},\sigma^{*}}({\sf F}{\sf G}\,\neg A_{i})\\
=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},s_{0},\sigma^{*}}({\sf F}^{\leq k}{\sf G}\,\neg A_{i})&\text{cont.\ measures}\\
\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},s_{0},\sigma^{*}}({\sf G}\,(\bigcap_{j>\max\{i,k\}}\neg A_{j}^{j}))&\text{by \eqref{eq:remove-k-prefix}}\\
=0&\text{by \eqref{eq:always-not-Tjj}}\end{array}\end{equation}Finally, we show that $\sigma^{*}$ is almost surely winning for
$\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$
from $s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$.
\@@amsalign
\end{proof}\par\par\par\begin{corollary}Consider a countable MDP ${\mathcal{M}}$ with initial state $s_{0}$ and a
$\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ objective.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@If ${\mathcal{M}}$ is universally transient then there exists a deterministic positional strategy that is optimal from every
state that admits an optimal strategy.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@In general, even if ${\mathcal{M}}$ is not universally transient,
if there exists an optimal strategy from $s_{0}$, then there
also exists an optimal deterministic Markov strategy.
\end{enumerate}\end{corollary}\begin{proof}Towards item 1.,
since ${\mathcal{M}}$ is universally transient and $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ is
shift invariant, it follows
from \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:optnestedbuchiunivtrans} and \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{as-to-opt} that there exists a single deterministic positional strategy that is
optimal from every state that has an optimal strategy.
\par Towards item 2.,
consider the MDP $S({\mathcal{M}})$ that is derived from ${\mathcal{M}}$ by encoding the step
counter from $s_{0}$ into the states; cf.~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:encodestep}.
$S({\mathcal{M}})$ is trivially universally transient.
We assume that there exists an optimal strategy from $s_{0}$.
Then we can use \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:optnestedbuchiunivtrans} on $S({\mathcal{M}})$ and \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{steptopoint}
to obtain an optimal deterministic Markov strategy from $s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$.
\end{proof}\par\par\vskip 12.0pt plus 4.0pt minus 4.0pt\par\par Deterministic Markov strategies as in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:GFsc} are not the only type of `simple’ optimal
strategies for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$. Alternatively, optimal strategies (if they exist) can be chosen
as positional randomized, i.e., one can trade the step counter for randomization.
We start by considering the special case of almost surely winning strategies.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\begin{theorem}Let ${\mathcal{M}}=(S,S_{C},S_{R},{\longrightarrow},P,r)$ be a
countable MDP
with initial state $s_{0}$
and a $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ objective.
If there exists an almost surely winning strategy from $s_{0}$,
then there also exists an almost surely winning
randomized positional strategy.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}W.l.o.g.\ one can assume that ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching, and
that every state admits an almost surely winning strategy.
A run satisfies $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$
iff it visits infinitely many transitions
in $A_{i}$ for every $i\in\mathbb{N}$.
We partition the state space into infinitely many finite regions
in the shape of expanding rings around the initial state $s_{0}$,
where membership in each ring
is defined via certain lower and upper bounds on the length of the shortest path from
$s_{0}$.
Inside each ring, we fix a randomized positional strategy
that is a weighted combination of countably infinitely many different
deterministic positional strategies.
Inside the $i$-th ring, the strategy focuses with high probability
on visiting a transition in $A_{i}$, such that $A_{i}$ is visited with probability
$\geq 1/2$ in the $i$-th ring.
However, for each other index $j\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{i\}$,
the strategy also retains some small fixed positive chance $q_{j}>0$ to visit
the set $A_{j}$.
The set of runs from $s_{0}$ can then be partitioned into the
following two types.
Non-transient runs stay inside some finite subspace forever.
Hence, for each $j\in\mathbb{N}$, one infinitely often gets the same
fixed small positive chance $q_{j}>0$ to visit $A_{j}$, and thus one visits each $A_{j}$
infinitely often almost surely.
Transient runs eventually leave every finite set forever, and thus
visit the $i$-th ring for every $i$. So, for each $i\in\mathbb{N}$, there
is a chance $\geq 1/2$ of visiting $A_{i}$.
\par In either of the two cases,
the set of runs that don't satisfy $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$
is a nullset, because the sets $A_{i}$ form a monotone decreasing chain.
\end{proof}\par\begin{proof}W.l.o.g.\ we can assume that ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:branchingreplacement}.
Also w.l.o.g.\ we can assume that every state in ${\mathcal{M}}$ has an almost surely
winning strategy, because otherwise it would suffice to consider a sub-MDP as follows:
Consider an almost surely winning strategy
$\sigma^{\prime}$ from $s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$.
Let $S^{\prime}\subseteq S$ be the subset of states that are visited
under $\sigma^{\prime}$. By restricting ${\mathcal{M}}$ to $S^{\prime}$, we obtain a sub-MDP
${\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$ where all states are almost surely winning for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
It now suffices to construct an almost surely winning
randomized positional strategy from $s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$, since the same
strategy is also almost surely winning from $s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$.
Thus, in the rest of the proof we can assume that every state in ${\mathcal{M}}$ has an almost surely
winning strategy.
\par Let $d(s_{1},s_{2})$ be the length of the shortest path from state
$s_{1}$ to state $s_{2}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$. For every finite set of states
$\hat{S}\subseteq S$ let
${\sf Bubble}_{n}(\hat{S})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\{s^{\prime}\mid\exists s\in\hat{S}.\ d(s,s^{\prime})\leq n\}$.
Since ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching, ${\sf Bubble}_{n}(\hat{S})$ is finite
for every finite set $\hat{S}$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}$.
\par All states in ${\mathcal{M}}$ are almost surely winning for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$
and $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}\subseteq{\sf F}\,A_{j}$ for every $j\geq 1$,
thus
it follows that all states in ${\mathcal{M}}$ are almost surely winning
for ${\sf F}\,A_{j}$.
By \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{KMST2020c}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, for every $j\geq 1$, there exists a \emph{uniform}
deterministic positional strategy $\tau^{j}$ (that thus does not
depend on the start state) that is a.s.\ winning for the reachability objective ${\sf F}\,A_{j}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$, i.e.,
\begin{equation}\forall s\,{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\tau^{j}}({\sf F}\,A_{j})=1\end{equation}\par We inductively construct an increasing (w.r.t.\ set inclusion) sequence
of sets of states $S_{i}$ with $i=-1,0,1,2,\dots$ such that
$S_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\emptyset$,
$S_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\{s_{0}\}$,
$S_{i+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\sf Bubble}_{n_{i+1}}(S_{i})$
(for $i\geq 0$ and suitably chosen numbers $n_{i}$),
randomized positional strategies $\sigma_{i}$
and MDPs ${\mathcal{M}}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{M}}$ and ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ derived from ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$
by fixing the choices according to $\sigma_{i+1}$ inside $S_{i+1}$
such that the following two properties hold:
\begin{equation}\forall i\,\forall s\,\exists\sigma^{\prime}\ {\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma^{\prime}}(\bigcap_{j\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{j})=1\end{equation}i.e., all states in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ are still a.s.\ winning, and
\begin{equation}\forall s\in S_{i-1}\ {\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i-1},s,\sigma_{i}}({\sf F}^{\leq n_{i}}A_{i})\geq 1/4\end{equation}For the base case of $i=0$ we have $S_{0}=\{s_{0}\}$
and ${\mathcal{M}}_{0}={\mathcal{M}}$. \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-1} holds by our assumption on
${\mathcal{M}}$ and \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-2} for $i=0$ is vacuously true, since $S_{-1}=\emptyset$.
\par For the inductive step $i\to i+1$ consider ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ and let $s\in S$.
By the induction hypothesis and \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-1} there is a strategy $\sigma^{\prime}$ such that
${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma^{\prime}}({\sf F}\,A_{i+1})\geq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma^{\prime}}(\bigcap_{j\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{j})=1$.
I.e., all states in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ are a.s.\ winning for the reachability objective
${\sf F}\,A_{i+1}$.
\par By \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{KMST2020c}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} there exists a \emph{uniform}
deterministic positional
strategy $\sigma_{i}^{i+1}$ (that thus does not
depend on the start state) that is a.s.\ winning for the reachability objective ${\sf F}\,A_{i+1}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$, i.e.,
$\forall s\in S\ {\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i}^{i+1}}({\sf F}\,A_{i+1})=1.$
By continuity of measures, we have $\forall s\in S$
$$1={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i}^{i+1}}({\sf F}\,A_{i+1})={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i}^{i+1}}\left(\cup_{m\geq 0}\,{\sf F}^{\leq m}A_{i+1}\right)=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i}^{i+1}}({\sf F}^{\leq m}A_{i+1}).$$Thus we can pick a number $m(s)\in\mathbb{N}$ such that
$${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i}^{i+1}}({\sf F}^{\leq m(s)}A_{i+1})\geq 1/2.$$(Note that $m(s)$ does depend on the start state $s$, unlike the
strategy $\sigma_{i}^{i+1}$.)
\par Since $S_{i}$ is finite, we can pick a finite number
$n_{i+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\max\{m(s)\mid s\in S_{i}\}\in\mathbb{N}$
and obtain
\begin{equation}\forall s\in S_{i}\ {\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i}^{i+1}}({\sf F}^{\leq n_{i+1}}A_{i+1})\geq 1/2.\end{equation}We let $S_{i+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\sf Bubble}_{n_{i+1}}(S_{i})$, and thus the above
is attained inside $S_{i+1}$.
\par We define the randomized positional strategy $\sigma_{i+1}$ on the set of states $S_{i+1}$
of ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ as follows.
(Note that in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ the choices in states $S_{i}$ are already fixed.)
At every state $s$ in $S_{i+1}\setminus S_{i}$ play like $\sigma_{i}^{i+1}$ with
some large probability $p_{i+1}$ (to be determined) and otherwise,
play like the
deterministic positional
strategy $\tau^{j}$
(from \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-uniform} above)
with a small
probability $(1-p_{i+1})\cdot 2^{-j}$ for all $j\geq 1$.
Since we have $p_{i+1}+(1-p_{i+1})\sum_{j\geq 1}2^{-j}=1$, this is a
distribution and the strategy $\sigma_{i+1}$ is well defined.
\par Intuitively, $\sigma_{i+1}$ focuses on the objective ${\sf F}\,A_{i+1}$
with a large probability $p_{i+1}$,
but keeps every other reachability objective ${\sf F}\,A_{j}$ in sight with a
nonzero probability.
Note that the attainment of $\sigma_{i+1}$ with respect to ${\sf F}^{\leq n_{i+1}}A_{i+1}$
is a continuous function in $p_{i+1}$, which converges to a value $\geq 1/2$ for
every start state $s\in S_{i}$ by \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-3}.
I.e.,
$$\forall s\in S_{i}\ \lim_{p_{i+1}\to 1}\ {\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i+1}}({\sf F}^{\leq n_{i+1}}A_{i+1})\geq 1/2.$$From the finiteness of $S_{i}$ it follows that there exists
a probability $p_{i+1}<1$ such that
$$\forall s\in S_{i}\ {\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i},s,\sigma_{i+1}}({\sf F}^{\leq n_{i+1}}A_{i+1})\geq 1/4$$and thus we obtain \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-2} for $i+1$.
\par We obtain the MDP ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ from ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$ by fixing all choices in
$S_{i+1}\setminus S_{i}$ according to $\sigma_{i+1}$.
\par Now we show that \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-1} holds for ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$.
We construct a strategy $\sigma^{\prime}$ that is a.s.\ winning for $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ in
${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ from every start state.
Inside $S_{i+1}$ all choices are already fixed in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$
according to strategies $\sigma_{k}$ for $1\leq k\leq i+1$.
By definition of these $\sigma_{k}$, for all $j\geq 1$ at each state $s\in S_{i+1}$
the strategy $\tau^{j}$ is played with some positive probability $p(s,j)>0$.
Then $p^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\min_{s\in S_{i+1}}p(s,j)>0$, since
$S_{i+1}$ is finite.
The strategy $\sigma^{\prime}$ plays in phases $j=1,2,3,\dots$.
In each phase $j$ it plays like $\tau^{j}$ everywhere outside of $S_{i+1}$
until $A_{j}$ is reached, and then proceeds to phase $j+1$, etc.
It suffices to show that in every phase $j$ we reach $A_{j}$ eventually almost surely,
i.e., we'll show that
\begin{equation}\forall s\ {\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1},s,\tau^{j}}({\sf F}\,A_{j})=1\end{equation}We partition the complement of the objective ${\sf F}\,A_{j}$
into two parts and show that each is a nullset.
We partition $\neg{\sf F}\,A_{j}$ into those runs that visit $S_{i+1}$ infinitely often
and those that don't.
\begin{equation}\neg{\sf F}\,A_{j}={\sf G}\neg A_{j}=({\sf G}\neg A_{j}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}S_{i+1})\uplus({\sf G}\neg A_{j}\cap{\sf F}{\sf G}\neg S_{i+1})\end{equation}\par For the first part of the partition, consider the strategy $\tau^{j}$ on ${\mathcal{M}}$.
By \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-uniform} and continuity of measures, for every state $s$ we have
$$1={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\tau^{j}}({\sf F}\,A_{j})=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\tau^{j}}({\sf F}^{\leq m}A_{j}).$$Thus for every state $s$ we can pick a number $m(s)$ such that
${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\tau^{j}}({\sf F}^{\leq m(s)}A_{j})\geq 1/2$.
Let $m\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\max_{s\in S_{i+1}}m(s)$. The number $m$ is finite, since
$S_{i+1}$ is finite.
Recall that in ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ the fixed strategy inside the finite set $S_{i+1}$ plays
$\tau^{j}$ with some probability $\geq p^{j}>0$ at each state in $S_{i+1}$.
Outside of $S_{i+1}$ we play $\tau^{j}$ with probability $1$ (and thus
also with probability $\geq p^{j}$).
It follows that, for every $s^{\prime}\in S_{i+1}$, we have
${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1},s^{\prime},\tau^{j}}({\sf F}^{\leq m}A_{j})\geq(p^{j})^{m}\cdot 1/2>0$.
Therefore
\begin{equation}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1},s,\tau^{j}}({\sf G}\neg A_{j}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,S_{i+1})\leq(1-(p^{j})^{m}\cdot 1/2)^{\infty}=0\end{equation}For the second part of the partition, consider an arbitrary state $s^{\prime}$. We
have
\begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1},s^{\prime},\tau^{j}}({\sf G}\neg A_{j}\cap{\sf G}\neg S_{i+1})={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s^{\prime},\tau^{j}}({\sf G}\neg A_{j}\cap{\sf G}\neg S_{i+1})\leq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s^{\prime},\tau^{j}}({\sf G}\neg A_{j})=0\end{array}\end{equation}The first equality above holds because ${\mathcal{M}}$ and ${\mathcal{M}}_{i+1}$ coincide outside
$S_{i+1}$.
The last equality is due to \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-uniform}.
Thus for all $s\in S$
\begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1},s,\tau^{j}}({\sf G}\neg A_{j}\cap{\sf F}{\sf G}\neg S_{i+1})\leq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1},s,\tau^{j}}({\sf F}({\sf G}\neg A_{j}\cap{\sf G}\neg S_{i+1}))&\text{set inclusion}\\
\leq\sup_{s^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1},s^{\prime},\tau^{j}}({\sf G}\neg A_{j}\cap{\sf G}\neg S_{i+1})&\text{def.\ of ${\sf F}$}\\
=0&\text{by \eqref{eq:partition-part-2-pre}}\end{array}\end{equation}By combining
\eqref{eq:partition-neg-Tj},
\eqref{eq:partition-part-1}
and
\eqref{eq:partition-part-2}
we obtain \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-as-j} and thus
\eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-1}.
This concludes the inductive construction.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0ptNow we construct the
randomized positional
strategy $\sigma$ that is almost surely winning for
$\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ from
$s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$.
The strategy $\sigma$ plays like $\sigma_{i+1}$ at all states in
$S_{i+1}\setminus S_{i}$ for all $i$, i.e., it corresponds exactly to the
choices that are fixed in the derived MDPs ${\mathcal{M}}_{i}$.
We consider the dual objective $\neg\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ and show that it is a
nullset under $\sigma$.
First we note that
\begin{equation}\neg\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i}.\end{equation}We show that each part of this union ${\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i}$ is a
nullset under $\sigma$.
To this end, we partition ${\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i}$ into two parts:
\begin{equation}{\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i}=(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\mathtt{Transience})\uplus(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\neg\mathtt{Transience})\end{equation}where
$\mathtt{Transience}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\bigcap_{s\in S}{\sf F}{\sf G}\neg s$.
\par For the first part of the partition of
\eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-part-transience},
we start by considering the slightly different objective
$({\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\mathtt{Transience}$.
Let $s^{\prime\prime}$ be an arbitrary state in ${\mathcal{M}}$ that is reachable from
$s_{0}$. Thus there exists some minimal index $k(s^{\prime\prime})$ such that
$s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{k(s^{\prime\prime})}$. Now consider only the set of runs ${\textit{Runs}_{{{\mathcal{M}}}}}{s^{\prime\prime}}$
that start from $s^{\prime\prime}$.
Transient runs eventually leave every finite set
forever. Thus
${\textit{Runs}_{{{\mathcal{M}}}}}{s^{\prime\prime}}\cap({\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\mathtt{Transience}\subseteq{\textit{Runs}_{{{\mathcal{M}}}}}{s^{\prime\prime}}\cap({\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\bigcap_{j\geq\max(i,k(s^{\prime\prime}))}{\sf F}(S_{j}\setminus S_{j-1})$.
On the other hand, by \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-2} and the definition of
$\sigma$ we have
$\forall s\in S_{j}\ {\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\sigma}({\sf F}^{\leq n_{j+1}}A_{j+1})\geq 1/4$.
I.e., $A_{j+1}$ is visited with probability $\geq 1/4$ inside $S_{j+1}$.
For the infinitely many $j\geq\max(i,k(s^{\prime\prime}))$
we have the inclusion $A_{i}\supseteq A_{j}$
and thus for every state $s^{\prime\prime}$ we have
$$\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s^{\prime\prime},\sigma}(({\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\mathtt{Transience})\\
\leq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s^{\prime\prime},\sigma}(({\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\bigcap_{j\geq\max(i,k(s^{\prime\prime}))}{\sf F}(S_{j}\setminus S_{j-1}))\leq(1-1/4)^{\infty}=0\end{array}$$Finally,
\begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\mathtt{Transience})\leq\sup_{s^{\prime\prime}}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s^{\prime\prime},\sigma}(({\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\mathtt{Transience})=0.\end{array}\end{equation}\par For the second part
of the partition of \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-part-transience},
for some arbitrary state $s$,
consider the event $({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap({\sf G}{\sf F}\ s)$.
Continuity of measures and \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-uniform} yield
$1={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\tau^{i}}({\sf F}\,A_{i})=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\tau^{i}}({\sf F}^{\leq m}A_{i}).$
Thus we can pick a number $m(s)$ such that
${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\tau^{i}}({\sf F}^{\leq m(s)}A_{i})\geq 1/2.$
Recall that $\sigma$ plays $\tau^{i}$ with some probability
$p^{i}(s^{\prime})>0$ at each state
$s^{\prime}\in{\sf Bubble}_{m(s)}(\{s\})$
that is reachable from $s$
in $\leq m(s)$ steps. Since ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching,
${\sf Bubble}_{m(s)}(\{s\})$ is finite
and thus $p^{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\min_{s^{\prime}\in{\sf Bubble}_{m(s)}(\{s\})}p^{i}(s^{\prime})>0.$
It follows that
$$q_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s,\sigma}({\sf F}^{\leq m(s)}A_{i})\geq(p^{i})^{m(s)}\cdot 1/2>0$$i.e., after every visit to $s$ we have a positive probability $q_{i}$ of reaching $A_{i}$.
Therefore for all states $s^{\prime\prime}$ we have
${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s^{\prime\prime},\sigma}(({\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap({\sf G}{\sf F}\ s))\leq(1-q_{i})^{\infty}=0.$
Thus,
\begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap({\sf G}{\sf F}\ s))\leq\sup_{s^{\prime\prime}}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s^{\prime\prime},\sigma}(({\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap({\sf G}{\sf F}\ s))=0.\end{array}\end{equation}From
the definition of $\mathtt{Transience}$, a union bound and \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-not-transient-part}
we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\neg\mathtt{Transience})={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\bigcup_{s\in S}{\sf G}{\sf F}\ s)\\
\leq\sum_{s\in S}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\ s)=0\end{array}\end{equation}\par Finally, we show that $\sigma$ wins almost surely.
$$\begin{array}[]{llr}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i})\geq 1-{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(\neg\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i})&\ \text{duality}\\
=1-{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(\bigcup_{i}{\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})&\ \text{\eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-limsup-char}}\\
\geq 1-\sum_{i}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})&\ \text{union bound}\\
=1-\sum_{i}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\mathtt{Transience})&\ \text{case split}\\
\quad-\sum_{i}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(({\sf F}{\sf G}\ \neg A_{i})\cap\neg\mathtt{Transience})\\
=1&\ \text{\eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-transient}, \eqref{eq:thm:mr-opt-pp-not-transient}}&\end{array}$$\end{proof}\par\begin{corollary}Consider a countable MDP ${\mathcal{M}}$ and a
$\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ objective.
There exists a single randomized positional
strategy that is optimal from every state that has an optimal strategy.
\end{corollary}\begin{proof}Since the objective $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ is shift invariant in every MDP,
the result follows from \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:nestedbuchimr} and
\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{as-to-opt}.
\end{proof}\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{\texorpdfstring{Strategy Complexity of $\eps$-optimal Strategies}
{Strategy Complexity of epsilon-optimal Strategies}
}
\par First we show a general result about a combined objective that includes
$\mathtt{Transience}$. It generalizes
the result on a combined $\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}A$ objective of
\cite[cite]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{KMST:Transient-arxiv}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma 4)}.
\par\vskip 12.0pt plus 4.0pt minus 4.0pt\begin{theorem}Consider a countable MDP ${\mathcal{M}}$ with initial state $s_{0}$ and the objective
${\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathtt{Transience}\cap\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
For every $\varepsilon>0$ there exists an $\varepsilon$-optimal deterministic 1-bit
strategy from $s_{0}$.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}By \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:branchingreplacement}, w.l.o.g.\ we can assume that ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching.
Since ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching, we can define
$d:S\to\mathbb{N}$ such that $d(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\min\{|w|:w\text{ is a path from $s_{0}$ to $s$}\}$.
Let ${\sf Bubble}_{n}(s_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\{t\in{\longrightarrow}_{{\mathcal{M}}}\mid t=(s{\longrightarrow}_{{\mathcal{M}}}s^{\prime}),\,d(s^{\prime})\leq n\}$.
Since ${\mathcal{M}}$ is finitely branching, ${\sf Bubble}_{n}(s_{0})$ is finite for every
$n\in\mathbb{N}$.
\par Let $A_{i}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}A_{i}\cap{\sf Bubble}_{n}(s_{0})$ and
let $A_{i}^{>n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}A_{i}\setminus A_{i}^{n}$.
(In particular, $A_{i}^{>0}=A_{i}$.)
\par Transient runs eventually leave every finite set forever, and in particular
each of the finite sets $A_{x}^{n}$ for every $x,n\in\mathbb{N}$.
Therefore, the objective ${\varphi}$ implies ${\sf F}(A_{x}^{>n})$
and thus ${\varphi}$ can be written as follows.
\begin{equation}{\varphi}=\mathtt{Transience}\cap\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}=\mathtt{Transience}\cap\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}\cap{\sf F}(A_{x}^{>n})\end{equation}\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{First step of the proof.}
We consider an $\varepsilon$-optimal strategy for
${\varphi}$ and show that it satisfies a certain stronger objective with
a probability that is almost as high (just losing one $\varepsilon$).
\par Let $\tau$ be a general $\varepsilon$-optimal strategy for ${\varphi}$ from $s_{0}$, i.e.,
\begin{equation}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}\left({\varphi}\right)\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-\varepsilon.\end{equation}We now show that
for a suitably chosen increasing sequence of natural numbers $0=n_{0}<n_{1}<n_{2}\dots$ we have
$${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}\left(\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{\infty}\left({\sf F}(A_{j}^{>n_{j-1}})\cap{\sf F}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})\right)\right)\cap{\varphi}\right)\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-2\varepsilon.$$To this end, we prove by induction on $i$ that
the following property holds for all $i\geq 0$ and
$\varepsilon_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\varepsilon\cdot 2^{-j}$.
\par{\bf\noindent Induction hypothesis:}
\begin{equation}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}\left(\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}\left({\sf F}(A_{j}^{>n_{j-1}})\cap{\sf F}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})\right)\right)\cap{\varphi}\right)\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-\varepsilon-\sum_{j=1}^{i}\varepsilon_{j}.\end{equation}\par\par{\bf\noindent Base case:}
For the base case of $i=0$, the property follows directly from
\eqref{eq:pointepsupper-eps}.
(Empty index sets for intersection and sum.)
\par{\bf\noindent Induction step from $i$ to $i+1$:}
By the induction hypothesis we have
$${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}\left(\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}\left({\sf F}(A_{j}^{>n_{j-1}})\cap{\sf F}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})\right)\right)\cap{\varphi}\right)\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-\varepsilon-\sum_{j=1}^{i}\varepsilon_{j}.$$Since, by \eqref{eq:acyclicity}, ${\varphi}={\varphi}\cap{\sf F}(A_{i+1}^{>n_{i}})$, we obtain that
$${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}\Bigg(\Bigg(\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}\left({\sf F}(A_{j}^{>n_{j-1}})\cap{\sf F}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})\right)\Bigg)\cap{\varphi}\cap{\sf F}(A_{i+1}^{>n_{i}})\Bigg)\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-\varepsilon-\sum_{j=1}^{i}\varepsilon_{j}.$$We have ${\sf F}(A_{i+1}^{>n_{i}})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf F}^{\leq k}(A_{i+1}^{>n_{i}})=\bigcup_{k>n_{i}}{\sf F}^{\leq k}(A_{i+1}^{>n_{i}})$.
Hence, by continuity of measures,
\@@amsalign
So there exists an $n_{i+1}>n_{i}$ such that
\@@amsalign
I.e., we get
$${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}\left(\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{i+1}\left({\sf F}(A_{j}^{>n_{j-1}})\cap{\sf F}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})\right)\right)\cap{\varphi}\right)\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-\varepsilon-\sum_{j=1}^{i+1}\varepsilon_{j}$$which completes the induction step.
\par By applying continuity of measures
to \eqref{eq:epsnestedbuchi-induction},
we obtain the desired result, i.e.,
\begin{equation}\begin{split}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}&\left(\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{\infty}\left({\sf F}(A_{j}^{>n_{j-1}})\cap{\sf F}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})\right)\right)\cap{\varphi}\right)\\
\geq&\ {\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-\varepsilon-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\varepsilon_{j}\\
=&\ {\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-2\varepsilon.\\
\end{split}\end{equation}\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Second step of the proof.}
Here we consider a stronger objective
$\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good}\subseteq{\varphi}$
and show how it relates to ${\varphi}$.
\par Let ${\sf Good}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}A_{i}^{n_{i}}$
be those transitions inside ${\sf Bubble}_{n_{i}}(s_{0})$ which are also in $A_{i}$.
Let ${\sf Good}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf Good}_{i}$.
\par The following claim shows that the inclusion
$\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good}\subseteq{\varphi}$
holds.
\par\begin{claim}$\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good}\subseteq\mathtt{Transience}\cap\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\varphi}$.
\end{claim}\begin{proof}Consider a run $\rho$ that satisfies $\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good}$.
In particular, $\rho$ must visit ${\sf Good}$ infinitely often.
However, ${\sf Good}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{i}^{n_{i}}$ where each set
$A_{i}^{n_{i}}$ is finite.
Since $\rho$ must also satisfy $\mathtt{Transience}$, it can visit each of these
finite sets $A_{i}^{n_{i}}$ only finitely often.
Therefore, $\rho$ must visit sets $A_{i}^{n_{i}}$ for arbitrarily large numbers
$i$.
That is, $\rho$ satisfies $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{j\geq i}{\sf F}\,A_{j}^{n_{j}}$
and thus also $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{j\geq i}{\sf F}\,A_{j}$.
Since the sets $\{A_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}$ are a decreasing chain, each visit to $A_{j}$
with $j\geq i$ is also a visit to $A_{i}$.
Hence $\rho$ also satisfies $\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$.
\end{proof}\par Next we show that the value of $s_{0}$ for
$\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good}$
is almost as high as its value for ${\varphi}$ (losing only $2\varepsilon$).
Due to the inclusions
$$\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{\infty}{\sf F}(A_{j}^{>n_{j-1}})\cap{\sf F}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})\right)\cap{\varphi}\subseteq{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good}\cap{\varphi}\subseteq\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good},$$it follows immediately from \eqref{eq:tauachievement} that
\begin{equation}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}(\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good})\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-2\varepsilon,\end{equation}and thus
\begin{equation}{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good}}(s_{0})}\geq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\tau}(\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good})\geq{\mathtt{val}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\varphi}}(s_{0})}-2\varepsilon.\end{equation}\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Third step of the proof.}
Here we put the previous steps together and show the statement of the theorem.
By \cite[cite]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{KMST:Transient-arxiv}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma 4)},
there exists an $\varepsilon$-optimal deterministic 1-bit strategy $\sigma^{*}$ for
$\mathtt{Transience}\cap{\sf G}{\sf F}\,{\sf Good}$ from $s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$. Thus
\@@amsalign
Since $\varepsilon>0$ can be made arbitrarily small, the result follows.
\end{proof}\par\begin{corollary}Consider a countable MDP ${\mathcal{M}}$ with initial state $s_{0}$ and a
$\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i}$ objective.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@If ${\mathcal{M}}$ is universally transient, then there exists a deterministic 1-bit strategy that is $\varepsilon$-optimal from $s_{0}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@In general, there exists a Det(SC+1-bit) $\varepsilon$-optimal strategy from
$s_{0}$ (even if ${\mathcal{M}}$ is not universally transient).
\end{enumerate}\end{corollary}\begin{proof}Towards item 1., if ${\mathcal{M}}$ is universally transient then, under every
strategy $\sigma$, we have
${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i})={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(\mathtt{Transience}\cap\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sf G}{\sf F}\,A_{i})$
and the result follows directly from \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:epsnestedbuchi}.
\par Towards item 2., consider the MDP $S({\mathcal{M}})$ that is derived from ${\mathcal{M}}$ by encoding the step
counter from $s_{0}$ into the states; cf.~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:encodestep}.
So $S({\mathcal{M}})$ is trivially universally transient.
Then we can use item 1.\ on $S({\mathcal{M}})$ to obtain a deterministic 1-bit strategy
on $S({\mathcal{M}})$. Finally, \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{steptopoint}
yields an $\varepsilon$-optimal Det(SC+1-bit) strategy from $s_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}$.
\end{proof}\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Lower Bounds}
We present the lower bounds on the strategy complexity in terms of
the objective $\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$, because it is much more intuitive to think of counterexamples
with rewards rather than thinking about which transitions are labeled as belonging to which set $A_{i}$.
The MDPs in this section all have transition based rewards.
\par We begin by presenting the results for optimal strategies.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{2}}{Det(F) strategies are not enough for almost sure $\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$ in finitely branching MDPs. Note that this is very similar to \cite[cite]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{Sudderth:2020}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Example 1)}.
}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 2}}{Det(F) strategies are not enough for almost sure $\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$ in finitely branching MDPs. Note that this is very similar to \cite[cite]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{Sudderth:2020}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Example 1)}.
}}}
\end{center}\end{figure}\par\vskip 12.0pt plus 4.0pt minus 4.0pt\begin{proposition}There exists a finitely branching countable MDP ${\mathcal{M}}$ with rational
rewards as in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{limsuplowerdetf}
with initial state $s_{0}$ such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\exists\hat{\sigma}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\hat{\sigma}}(\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0))=1$,
i.e., the state $s_{0}$ is almost surely winning for $\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@For every Det(F) strategy $\sigma$ we have ${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\sigma}(\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0))=0.$
\end{enumerate}So almost surely winning strategies, when they exist, cannot be chosen Det(F).
\end{proposition}\par\begin{proof}Towards \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:ppalmostlowerstep-1}, let
$\hat{\sigma}$ be the deterministic strategy that turns off the ladder
(i.e., goes back to $s_{0}$) at state $r_{i}$ upon visiting it for the
first time and then never again thereafter. Note that $\hat{\sigma}$
uses infinite memory.
Let ${\mathfrak{R}}$ be the set of all runs generated by $\hat{\sigma}$.
We want to show that ${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},\hat{\sigma},s_{0}}({\mathfrak{R}}\wedge\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0))=1$.
Since $\hat{\sigma}$ is deterministic and all states in ${\mathcal{M}}$ are
controlled, we have $|{\mathfrak{R}}|=1$.
Therefore it suffices to show that the unique $\rho\in{\mathfrak{R}}$ is winning.
By definition of $\hat{\sigma}$, $\rho$ sees every reward $\frac{1}{i}$
exactly once for $i=1,2,3,\dots$.
Hence the $\limsup$ of the payoffs that $\rho$ sees is $\limsup_{i\to\infty}-\dfrac{1}{i}=0$
and thus $\rho\in\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$ and
${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},s_{0},\hat{\sigma}}(\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0))=1$.
\par Towards \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:ppalmostlowerstep-2},
let $\sigma$ be a deterministic strategy with $k$ memory modes $\{0,1,\dots,k-1\}$.
For each memory mode ${\sf m}\in\{0,1,\dots,k-1\}$,
consider $\sigma$'s behavior from $s_{0}$.
Since $\sigma$ is deterministic, there are only two cases.
In the first case we never visit $s_{0}$ again.
In the second case we deterministically take a transition $r_{i}{\longrightarrow}s_{0}$ for some
particular $i({\sf m})$ that depends on the initial memory mode ${\sf m}$.
For runs of the first case the $\limsup$ of the payoffs is $-1$.
For runs of the second case, we never visit states $r_{j}$ for
$j>i_{\max}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\max_{{\sf m}\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}}i({\sf m})$ and thus the $\limsup$
of the payoffs is $\leq-\frac{1}{i_{\max}}<0$.
Thus no runs induced by $\sigma$ satisfy $\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$
and ${\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}},\sigma,s_{0}}(\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0))=0$.
\end{proof}\par\vskip 12.0pt plus 4.0pt minus 4.0pt\par Now we present the lower bound results for $\varepsilon$-optimal strategy complexity.
\par The $\limsup_{\it DP}(\geq 0)$ objective generalizes the B\"{u}chi objective ${\sf G}{\sf F}\,A$, even for
integer rewards, hence the lower bounds for $\varepsilon$-optimal strategies for ${\sf G}{\sf F}\,A$ from
\cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{KMST:ICALP2019,KMSW2017}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} carry over. The following \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:ppepstep} shows that
randomized strategies with finite memory are not sufficient.
